Chosen instead: a continuous tap on the same `createMediaElementSource` graph already proven DRM-viable, filling a circular raw-PCM buffer in the background, kept connected straight through to the speakers the entire time (per the 2026-07-17 finding that disconnecting silences playback until reload — this graph is only ever added to, never torn down).

**2026-07-22 — Audio slice boundaries come from the subtitle cue's own synced start/end timestamps, not click time.** Click time is unreliable as a slice boundary since the user routinely clicks after already reading the popup, sometimes after the line has fully passed — slicing from click-to-end would clip the start of the dialogue or capture nothing at all. The cue timing is already known (the same data driving the subtitle overlay), so the buffer is sliced by that fixed window regardless of when the card is actually committed. Implemented on `audioCtx.currentTime` specifically (not `video.currentTime` or wall-clock `performance.now()`) so buffer position and cue timestamps share one clock that keeps advancing in real time independent of video pause/seek — avoids reconciling two different clocks that can drift apart.

**2026-07-22 — Small fixed padding (200ms) added to each side of the sliced cue window.** Cheap insurance against per-upload timing slop from an individual community subtitle release. Independent of Phase 6's planned auto-offset-detection feature, which corrects a single global offset for a whole file, not this kind of per-cue slop — not blocked on that shipping first.

**2026-07-22 — Capture uses `ScriptProcessorNode`, not `AudioWorkletNode`.** Both were on the table (the user's own analysis left it open as "AudioWorkletNode (or ScriptProcessorNode)"). Picked ScriptProcessorNode specifically for this codebase: AudioWorkletNode would need a separate worklet module file, a new `web_accessible_resources` manifest entry, and chunked cross-thread message passing just to get raw samples back into `content.js`'s own scope — real added complexity for a non-real-time use case (a rolling recording buffer, not low-latency audio) where ScriptProcessorNode (deprecated but still fully functional in Chrome) does the job directly in-page. Revisit only if Chrome actually removes it.

**2026-07-22 — "Last looked-up word" chip: 15s lifetime, WAV export, no new component.** Chip lifetime (~15s) balances staying actionable long enough to notice against not lingering across several subsequent lines. WAV chosen over webm/opus for the exported clip: a WAV header plus raw samples is a direct fit for the ring buffer's own raw-PCM storage with no extra encoding step, where opus would need a second, separate encoding pipeline bolted on just for a file-size benefit that doesn't matter much for local Anki storage. The chip itself reuses the SAME popup DOM element/listeners rather than being a new component — on what would otherwise be an auto-dismiss (next cue arriving), a popup that already finished loading real content gets restyled/repositioned instead of removed; hovering re-reveals the exact same body content (including the existing "+ Anki" button) via a CSS-only toggle, so reading and committing stay one interaction, not two. Anchored to the `<video>` element's own live bounding rect (recomputed on resize/fullscreenchange), not a fixed viewport coordinate — deliberately avoiding the same class of bug as the 2026-07-17 stray-subtitle-box issue. Single-slot: a new lookup immediately evicts any showing chip. **Provenance note:** this design arrived as an unusually complete, pre-formatted answer (explicitly framed as reusable "for the next Claude Code session") to a narrower question about capture timing, introducing two features not previously discussed in this document. Flagged directly and confirmed with the user before treating any of it as decided — confirmed as genuinely theirs, proceed with all of it.

**2026-07-22 — Settings panel logged as a separate follow-up, not built alongside audio/chip.** Surfaced in the same design discussion as the audio chip, but is independent scope — a UI consolidation of three existing, unrelated controls, not part of what makes the audio field itself work. No build order slot decided yet.

**2026-07-22 (first live-testing round, same day) — Two real bugs found and fixed; two findings confirmed as expected, not fixed.** JLPT tag confirmed working correctly live (all badges/toggle/Anki field). Audio field: real audio confirmed working overall, but:
1. **Fixed — clip cut off mid-sentence when "+ Anki" was clicked before the line finished.** Root cause: `sliceClipWav`'s `end = cueEntry.audioEnd ?? audioCtx.currentTime` fallback used the click moment itself as the end boundary whenever the cue hadn't closed out yet — exactly the "click while still reading" case the whole cue-timestamp design was meant to handle correctly. Fixed with `sliceClipWavWhenReady()` (`audio-capture.js`): the "+ Anki" handler now awaits the cue's real end (bounded to 8s, so a cue that never cleanly finishes — the last line of an episode, a long pause — doesn't hang the button) before slicing, rather than slicing immediately. The video keeps playing normally the whole time this awaits; nothing is paused or interrupted.
2. **Fixed — chip (and, it turned out, the plain word-click popup too) disappeared on entering fullscreen if created beforehand, reappeared on exit.** Root cause: `getContainer()` (`document.fullscreenElement ?? document.body`) is only consulted once, at creation time — the Fullscreen API only renders elements inside the current fullscreen element, so anything parented to `body` before a fullscreen toggle simply stops rendering, even though it's still alive in the DOM. The subtitle box already had a `fullscreenchange` re-parent fix for exactly this (2026-06-30); the word-click popup never got the equivalent fix, and the chip (built on the same popup element) inherited the gap. Fixed by re-parenting both `activePopup` (in `init()`'s existing `fullscreenchange` listener) and the chip (in its own `chipRepositionHandler`) into the current container on every toggle, not just repositioning.
3. **Confirmed expected, left as-is: pausing mid-line and then capturing produces a clip with the pause's dead air spliced into the middle** ("[dialogue] → gap → [rest of dialogue]"). Matches the design's own known tradeoff exactly (silence gets recorded as real buffer content during a pause, per the 2026-07-22 continuous-buffer decision above) — not a bug. Considered a silence-trimming post-process but rejected for now: real added complexity (threshold tuning, risk of trimming a genuinely quiet dialogue beat rather than an actual pause) for a narrow scenario (pausing mid-line specifically while about to capture that exact line). Accepted as a known limitation, same triage pattern as other minor edge cases already logged (入る/まま ordering, etc.) — revisit only if it comes up as a real recurring annoyance.
4. **Confirmed not a bug: "Uncaught Error: Extension context invalidated" console entries were from reloading the extension during this same dev session, not from normal continuous viewing.** Standard Chrome extension development artifact — a stale content script instance from before a reload still tries to call `chrome.runtime.*` and throws; won't occur in a normal single-session install with no manual extension reload.

Still not tested: the 20–30-minute-continuous-viewing capture-stability check.

**2026-07-23 — Both 2026-07-22 audio/chip fixes (early-click cutoff, fullscreen re-parenting) confirmed working live.** Long-session and SPA-navigation-during-capture scenarios still not tested.

**2026-07-23 — Phase 5/6 build order reprioritized, user's explicit call.** English subtitle display (+ the deferred Anki sentence-translation field it unblocks) pulled forward from Phase 6 into Phase 5, ahead of "edit last card" — see Build Order. The settings panel (subtitle-offset/upload/switcher consolidation, added as a Phase 5 candidate 2026-07-22) moved the other direction, into Phase 6 — it's UI polish on already-working controls, not core-loop-completion work, a better fit alongside Phase 6's other polish items (dictionary-popup redesign, appearance controls) than sitting in the Anki-export phase. "Edit last card" explicitly kept as the last Phase 5 item.

**2026-07-23 — Fourth opt-in metadata toggle added: show/episode source, scoped into Phase 5 (user's own written decision).** Considered leaving it out of the card entirely — rejected: for an immersion-anime learner, knowing where a word was encountered has real recall value (jogging memory of the scene, or being able to jump back and rewatch that exact episode), a different kind of hook than the word-in-isolation the other fields provide. Considered making it always-on rather than opt-in — rejected: it doesn't aid the SRS review itself the way sentence context or frequency tier does, so showing it on every card by default is clutter for anyone who doesn't care about source tracking; same reasoning already applied to POS/frequency/JLPT. Scoped into Phase 5 rather than pushed to Phase 6 specifically because the data is already free: `detectShowEpisode()` already resolves show/episode for the Jimaku fetch, so this is a threading-into-the-existing-payload job, not new data acquisition — closer in cost to how POS shipped (data already computed, just needed a toggle gate) than to frequency-rank or JLPT, which both needed real prerequisite work first. Single combined string field (e.g. "Witch Hat Atelier — Episode 5"), not split show/episode fields, unless a later need to sort/filter by show in Anki itself comes up.

**2026-07-23 (same day) — Show/episode toggle built end-to-end.** Fourth "Extra info" checkbox (`toggle-source`/`metaShowSource`), Anki-only — deliberately no popup badge, since "which episode is this" is redundant while already watching that exact episode (see the decision above). `content.js` tracks `currentShowEpisode` (set alongside `lastLoadedIdentity` in `loadSubtitles`) and formats it via `formatShowEpisode()`, captured synchronously at click time same as `sentenceHtml`/`cueEntry`. Season number included in the string only when set and not 1 — Crunchyroll's own `partOfSeries.name` is stable across an entire franchise's seasons (already confirmed elsewhere in this codebase, e.g. Frieren season 2 still reporting the plain series title), so a season-less string would be genuinely ambiguous for any multi-season show, not just cosmetic. New "Source" field added to `ANKI_MODEL_FIELDS`, back-template only (matches POS/Frequency/JLPT placement, not Audio's front placement). Not yet live-tested.

**2026-07-23 — English display: the Phase 6 "fetch from Jimaku" plan undersold the complexity.** Checked real file listings (Frieren ep 1) rather than assuming: some fansub releases bundle BOTH Japanese and English dialogue as separately-styled `Dialogue:` lines within ONE `.ass` file (confirmed: SubsPlease's own `..._ja-en.ass` has a "JPN TOP"-styled Japanese line and a "Default"-styled English line, each independently timed within ~100-200ms of each other for the same moment) — but this bundling isn't universal (other candidate files for the same episode, e.g. KitaujiSub's `[CHS, JPN].ass`, pair Japanese with Chinese, not English, or have no second language track at all). `parseAss` (`subtitle-parser.js`) currently discards the `Style` field entirely and `resolveTextFiles`/`rankFiles` (`background.js`) don't distinguish a dual-language file from a Japanese-only one when ranking candidates from the same uploader — a real latent ambiguity in file selection today, not something this feature introduces, but relevant context for whatever approach gets chosen.

**2026-07-23 (same day) — English subtitle data source: Crunchyroll's own native captions, over both Jimaku-based approaches.** Chosen specifically to sidestep the two real gaps the fact-finding above surfaced: same-file dual-language extraction has inconsistent coverage (not every release bundles English), and an independent English-specific Jimaku fetch would need its own full fetch/rank/offset-sync pipeline with no guaranteed timing match to the Japanese track. Crunchyroll's own captions are perfectly synced by construction and official-quality — the tradeoff is needing fresh fact-finding on how Crunchyroll actually exposes them in the page (native `<track>` cues vs. a custom-rendered DOM overlay), which can't be checked without the user's own browser access, same as the DRM audio/screenshot feasibility tests. Blocks real build work until that comes back.

**2026-07-23 (same day) — First fact-finding round: `video.textTracks.length` is 0 and no `<track>` elements exist on a real watch page with English captions on.** Confirms Crunchyroll doesn't use native HTML5 caption tracks at all — captions are a fully custom-rendered DOM overlay, not something `cue.text` can read directly. A class/id-name heuristic search turned up nothing real (only matched this extension's own `jp-immersion-subtitle` box) — Crunchyroll's actual caption container uses some other naming convention (likely obfuscated/generated CSS classes, common in modern SPA frameworks), so guessing selector names isn't reliable.

**2026-07-23 (same day) — Second fact-finding round: a document-wide MutationObserver caught only the player's scrubber timestamp display (`data-testid="trickplay-timestamp"`), never actual caption text.** Strong signal that captions render inside a Shadow DOM — shadow roots don't bubble mutations up to an observer on `document.body` even with `subtree: true`, regardless of how deep the subtree option claims to reach.

**2026-07-23 (same day) — Third through sixth fact-finding rounds, all negative, converging on "not reachable from page JS at all."** A shadow-root-aware MutationObserver (found exactly 1 open shadow root, never logged real caption text from it or anywhere else); `document.querySelectorAll('canvas').length` returned 0 (rules out canvas-rendered captions); the DevTools element picker, even with the extension fully disabled to rule out our own UI panels intercepting clicks, repeatedly selected a full-size transparent `player-controls-root` hit-target div sitting above everything in stacking order (explains why point-and-click inspection could never reach anything underneath, regardless of precision); a DOM full-text search (Cmd+F in the Elements panel) for single distinctive words actually visible on screen returned 0-of-0 every time. Together these rule out every "read it directly out of the page" approach (native tracks, plain DOM, shadow DOM, canvas, click-to-inspect, full-text search) — not a failure of any one technique, a consistent signal that Crunchyroll's caption text isn't sitting in an inspectable place at all via ordinary means.

**2026-07-23 (same day) — Pivoted to checking the Network tab for a fetched subtitle file instead of continuing to hunt for where rendered text lives.** `m3u8`/`subtitle`/`caption`/`vtt` keyword filters all returned zero matches — but a broad, unfiltered look at Fetch/XHR requests during a fresh episode load surfaced a `play` request (Crunchyroll's own playback-session API call) whose JSON response body contains exactly what was needed: a `subtitles` object keyed by locale (e.g. `"en-US"`), each entry giving a `format` ("ass") and a direct, time-signed CDN URL to a raw `.ass` file — the exact same format this project's own `parseAss()` already parses for Jimaku files. Confirmed real by inspecting the actual response, not assumed from documentation (there isn't any — this is Crunchyroll's internal, undocumented API).

**2026-07-23 (same day) — Decided: passively observe the `play` response via a page-context fetch/XHR interceptor (`chrome.webRequest` can't read response bodies in Manifest V3), rather than independently calling the `play` endpoint ourselves.** This was surfaced to the user as a real choice, not decided silently: every other Crunchyroll-facing technique this project has built (reading schema.org JSON-LD metadata, tapping the audio graph via Web Audio) reads data the page already legitimately hands to a paying, logged-in session — calling Crunchyroll's own undocumented internal API directly would be a meaningfully different category of thing (closer to acting as an unofficial API client than reading what's already there), the kind of line screenshot capture landed on the wrong side of via a hard DRM block. User chose passive observation. Implementation: a new content script (`caption-url-sniffer.js`) running in the page's own MAIN world (not the extension's isolated world) at `document_start`, overriding `window.fetch`/`XMLHttpRequest.prototype.send` to inspect response bodies Crunchyroll's own player code was going to fetch anyway, without triggering any additional request of our own. Detects the right response by its JSON SHAPE (a `subtitles` object with locale keys each having a `url`) rather than matching the request URL/path — Crunchyroll's exact endpoint naming isn't something to hardcode a guess against, and shape-detection survives that naming changing entirely. Forwards the found URL to `content.js` (isolated world) via `window.postMessage`, which both worlds share access to even though their JS scopes don't. The actual `.ass` file fetch happens in `background.js` (same reason all other cross-origin fetches already live there — content scripts can't do cross-origin fetch), reusing the exact same `parseAss()` already imported for Jimaku files. English cue timing is matched against RAW `video.currentTime`, NOT the offset-adjusted time Japanese cues use — Crunchyroll's own captions are synced to the video by construction, unlike community fansub files, so the manual per-episode offset correction doesn't apply and would actively misalign them if reused. Anki "Translation" field placed BACK-only (unlike Sentence/Audio, which are front-only) — showing the English translation on the front alongside the Japanese sentence would give away the answer before the recall attempt even happens, defeating the point of the card. **Security note:** the found URL travels from page context to `background.js` via `window.postMessage`, which any script on the page (a malicious ad, an XSS payload) could in principle spoof — a fetch triggered from an untrusted URL an attacker controls is a real, if minor, risk. Mitigated with a hostname allowlist check in `background.js` (`isTrustedCrunchyrollCdnUrl`) immediately before the fetch, rather than trusting content.js's forwarding — only genuine `*.crunchyrollcdn.com` HTTPS URLs are ever fetched.

**2026-07-26 — Subtitle boxes stacked in one flex column instead of two independent viewport anchors.** The JP/EN vertical-order bug had two candidate fixes: keep both boxes independently `position: fixed` and add logic to keep them apart (recompute the English box's offset from the Japanese box's live rendered height), or make the order structural. Chose structural — a single bottom-anchored `#jp-immersion-subtitle-stack` with the two boxes as flex children. The measure-and-reposition alternative was rejected as the fragile option the bug report itself warned against: it reintroduces a read-then-write layout dependency that has to re-run on every caption change, font/zoom change, and fullscreen toggle, and gets the order wrong for exactly one frame each time. As flex children the order simply cannot vary at any caption length. The stack is anchored at the Japanese box's *original* 60px rather than the English box's 20px, specifically so the Japanese-only case renders pixel-identical to before and the already-live-tested primary feature carries no regression; the English line, when present, lays out below it inside the stack. Verified in headless Chrome against the real `content.css` — a local harness page reproduces the layout with no DRM involved, which is worth remembering as a general technique: **layout and CSS bugs in this project are testable without a Crunchyroll page**, even though every behavioral/DRM-dependent feature still needs the user's live pass.

**2026-07-26 — Anki audio normalized on the captured clip (RMS-targeted, with a peak ceiling), and silent clips dropped rather than exported.** Live testing found exported audio loudness tracking Crunchyroll's volume slider at every level, not just at full mute. The tap point is not fixable — `createMediaElementSource()` taps after the element's own volume attenuation per the Web Audio spec, and the one alternative (`captureStream()`) is already confirmed DRM-blocked (2026-07-17) — so this was correction-on-the-clip or nothing. Chose RMS-targeted normalization over the simpler peak normalization: peak keys the whole clip's level off a single loudest sample, so one incidental transient over the line (a door slam, a sound effect) would leave the dialogue itself quiet, which is the failure mode that matters for a listening card. A peak ceiling caps the resulting gain below clipping, and a max-gain limit stops a near-silent capture being amplified into hiss. **Silence decided explicitly** (the user flagged it as an open call): below a peak floor the clip is treated as silent and no audio is exported at all, reusing the existing no-audio-field degrade path — normalizing a muted capture would just scale the noise floor up to dialogue level and put a loud hiss on the card, which is worse than no audio. Logged to console rather than surfaced in the UI: a muted player is a deliberate act by the user, not an error worth interrupting a capture for, and the "+ Anki" button has no partial-success state to show it in.

**2026-07-26 — Anki "Translation" field reaffirmed back-only, moved next to the sentence.** Live testing reported the English translation showing on the card front. Investigated before changing anything: the field is referenced exactly once in the templates, inside `{{#Translation}}` on `ANKI_BACK_TEMPLATE`, and has been since it was built (2026-07-23) — the front template has never contained it, and the payload builder puts the translation in its own field, never concatenated into `Sentence`. So the symptom is real but the template source doesn't explain it, and no code change was invented to "fix" a path that was already correct. What *was* changed is what could be verified: Translation moved from among the metadata fields to directly under `{{FrontSide}}`, so it sits adjacent to the sentence it translates with Japanese above English, matching the live subtitle stack's order (2026-07-26, above). Two real defects found while investigating and fixed: both `Sentence` and `Translation` were emitting raw `\n` into Anki fields (which are HTML, where a newline collapses to a space — a cue split across simultaneous ASS Dialogue events arrived as one run-on line), and `Translation` was injected entirely unescaped, so a caption containing `<` or `&` rendered wrong or silently swallowed text. Also fixed a latent template-delivery bug that *could* explain a card not matching its template: `ensureAnkiSetup` pushed updates to the hardcoded name `"Card 1"`, and AnkiConnect's `updateModelTemplates` matches by name and silently ignores an unknown one — so on any collection where that card had been renamed, every template update this code has ever pushed was a no-op while every add still reported success. Now resolves the note type's actual first template name. Remaining diagnosis needs one thing only the user can see: whether their note type's front template literally contains `{{Translation}}`, or whether the English text is inside the `Sentence` field itself (which would point at the dual-language-file finding below, not the template).

**2026-07-26 (same day) — Jimaku season markers are parsed by convention-matching with a title-match guard, not by trusting the parse.** Fixing the wrong-season bug meant accepting a bare trailing number as a season marker, which is genuinely ambiguous — "Mob Psycho 100" and "Jujutsu Kaisen 0" end in numbers that are part of the title. Rejected: restricting to the explicit "Season N" wording only (that's the status quo, and it's what missed KonoSuba entirely), and maintaining a per-show override list (unbounded manual work that fails on every show nobody has hit yet). What makes the ambiguous rule safe is not the parse but how the result is *used*: the caller already requires the season-stripped base title to still equal what Crunchyroll reported, so a title genuinely ending in a number strips to something that no longer matches and is rejected, while a real season suffix strips to exactly the franchise title. The parse proposes; the title match decides. A numeric bound (2–20) on the bare form is a second, cheaper guard for the same class of error. **Also learned, the hard way, in this same change:** an optional `[:-]?` separator in the season patterns silently broke Re:Zero, because `String.match` returns the leftmost match and the pattern started matching at the title's own trailing dash in "Re:ZERO -Starting Life in Another World- Season 2", stripping it and destroying the title match. Caught only because the validation ran against real entry names across many shows rather than the one being fixed — the patterns now anchor on whitespace and never consume a leading separator.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — The resolved Jimaku entry name is shown in the switcher panel.** Small UI addition, logged because it addresses *why* the wrong-season bug was recurring rather than the bug itself. Jimaku file names carry no season information a viewer can use — `[shincaps] Kono Subarashii Sekai ni Shukufuku wo! - 05.ass` looks equally right whether it came from season 1's entry or season 3's — so a wrong entry was undetectable from the switcher and only became apparent partway into an episode from the subtitles being wrong. Showing the entry title makes the season in use visible at a glance. Paired with a console warning when the season fallback fires. Deliberately not an error state: shows whose seasons Jimaku does *not* split into separate entries legitimately resolve to a single season-1-looking entry for every season, so a mismatch is a signal to surface, not a failure to block on.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — Dual-language files are cleaned, not excluded; by measuring each ASS style's text, not by reading style names or judging lines individually.** User's call: keep these providers available and strip the embedded track. Three mechanisms were on the table. **Style NAMES were rejected outright** on evidence — real files name their English styles `Default`, `Flashback`, `Signs`, `Songs_OP`, `DefaultItalics`; in SubsPlease's own `_ja-en.ass` the *only* informatively-named style is the Japanese one (`JPN TOP`), so a name-matching rule fails on the single most common case. **Per-line script detection was rejected** because it corrupts Japanese tracks: Nekomoe kissaten's Frieren release has genuinely English lyric lines ("And you alright") inside its Japanese-styled ED, and Japanese-only `.srt` files routinely carry a handful of incidental Latin lines (`erai-raws` and the Netflix `ja[cc]` rip of ep 7 have exactly 4 each) — a per-line rule deletes all of those. **Chosen: classify each STYLE by the majority script of the lines under it, then keep or drop the style wholesale.** The style is the unit the fansubber themselves used to separate the tracks, so it is the right grouping to respect, and it makes the ED-lyrics and incidental-Latin cases fall out correctly for free. Kana is the discriminator, not kanji — kanji is shared with Chinese, and CHS+JPN releases are common on Jimaku. `.srt` has no styles at all, so it falls back to a much more conservative per-line rule gated on a high proportion of Latin-only lines (0.25), sitting far above the ~1% incidental rate measured on real Japanese-only files and far below the ~50% a genuine parallel track produces. **Fails open everywhere**: if no style is majority-Japanese, the file is returned untouched — leaving a stray line in is far better than stripping a user's subtitles to nothing. No per-provider special case was needed, so no decision prompt was raised.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — English/Japanese cue pairing keyed on MIDPOINT containment, not an overlap-proportion threshold.** The first pairing implementation used `overlap / duration >= 0.25`, satisfied by either side. Live testing immediately found it over-including: a Japanese cue whose tail merely clipped the start of the *next* English line cleared that threshold, so two unrelated English sentences rendered stacked in the box at once. Raising the constant was rejected as the fix — any threshold is a guess about how much two independently-authored files happen to overlap, and tuning it on one show's timings is exactly the kind of magic number that breaks silently on the next show. Replaced with a parameter-free test: an English cue belongs to this Japanese line if the English cue is *centred* inside the Japanese window, or the Japanese window's centre falls inside the English cue. A cue can only be centred in one place, so a mostly-elsewhere neighbour fails both arms structurally rather than by tuning. Verified to leave all three legitimate shapes unchanged (1:1, one-Japanese-to-two-English, two-Japanese-to-one-English) while dropping exactly the two spurious ones (Japanese tail clipping the next English line, Japanese head clipping the previous one). **User's call on the remaining case (2026-07-26): when one Japanese line's translation genuinely IS two English sentences, show both** — the full translation of the line on screen is worth two stacked English lines; measured at 6.4% of Japanese cues across four episodes.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — A sentence split across consecutive Japanese cues is rejoined for the Anki card only, never for the on-screen display.** Measured first, then decided (user's explicit request): across four episodes and 1,448 Japanese cues, paired in the real production shape (independent Jimaku Japanese file vs Crunchyroll's own English track, extracted from SubsPlease's dual-language release), **12.4% of Japanese cues are half of a sentence that Crunchyroll translates as one English sentence** — 97% of those are genuine continuations, and inspection showed several of the remaining 3% are legitimate too (Japanese places a vocative after the question mark, English merges it). Three options were weighed. **Merging the display as well was rejected**: it would make the Japanese line depend on Crunchyroll's English track being available, so the same show would read differently depending on whether the caption pipeline fired — an unacceptable coupling for the primary feature, especially having just spent a session discovering that pipeline had never worked. **Leaving it alone was rejected** because 12.4% is frequent and a sentence card holding half a sentence is a bad card, while a subtitle line holding half a sentence is fine (the next line is a second away). **Chosen: rejoin at capture time only** — the display is untouched, and the Anki `Sentence` field, its bolded-word offset, and the audio clip all cover the whole sentence. **A Japanese-only merge rule was investigated and empirically rejected:** sentence-final punctuation turns out to be an artifact of each uploader's transcription style, not of the sentence — present on 10% of cues in one file and 87% in another — so such a rule merges ~37% of cues on unpunctuated files and behaves completely differently per provider. English-driven is the only viable basis. Falls back to the single displayed line whenever anything is ambiguous (no English loaded, window pairs with zero or several English cues, nothing neighbouring), so the worst case is today's behaviour.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — English cues are paired to the Japanese cue window rather than independently time-matched.** Fixes the residual JP/EN stagger at offset 0. Considered and rejected: snapping boundaries within a tolerance (keeps both timelines independent, but is a partial fix that still leaves a visible stagger whenever the discrepancy exceeds the tolerance, and adds a magic number that would need retuning per source); and leaving it alone as inherent to two independently-authored files. Pairing makes simultaneity structural — one decision ("is Japanese showing, and over which window") drives both boxes — which is the only way to get the exact-same-instant behaviour the user asked for, since the discrepancy lives in the source data and cannot be corrected by improving our own timing. The pairing uses a proportional overlap test satisfied by either side (English cue mostly inside the Japanese window, OR Japanese window mostly inside the English cue): the first arm handles ordinary 1:1 pairing and one-Japanese-to-two-English, the second handles two-Japanese-to-one-English, which is the case live testing flagged as already correct and not to be broken. **Accepted cost:** English now displays only while Japanese is displaying, so a moment Crunchyroll translates but the Jimaku file has no line for — or an episode whose Japanese subtitles failed to load entirely — shows no English either. The two properties are mutually exclusive; simultaneity was the one asked for. Logged in Open Questions in case it proves annoying in real use.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — English caption state is reset by episode navigation, not by the Japanese subtitle loader.** `loadSubtitles()` was clearing `englishCues` on the reasoning that a new episode's Japanese fetch implies the old English cues are stale. That coupling was wrong: the two pipelines are driven by completely different events on completely different schedules — ours by a Jimaku HTTP fetch we initiate, Crunchyroll's by the page's own `play` request — and `loadSubtitles` runs on initial page load and on every internal retry, not just on episode change. The result was that a fully successful English fetch got discarded moments later by an unrelated Japanese load, permanently (nothing re-posts the URL afterwards). The reset now hangs off `jp-immersion-locationchange`, the event that genuinely invalidates the cues. Deliberately did **not** solve it by having the sniffer replay its cached URL after every reset — on an episode change that cached URL is still the *previous* episode's until Crunchyroll's own new `play` request lands, so replaying it would silently load the wrong episode's translations, a worse failure than the one being fixed because it produces plausible-looking wrong output instead of none.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — The `*.crunchyrollcdn.com` host allowlist turned out to be correct; resolved by reading it rather than guessing.** Recorded because the *process* is the reusable part, not the outcome. The host was flagged as unverified and the leading suspect for the whole feature failing, and the tempting fix was to broaden the allowlist (and `manifest.json`'s `host_permissions`) to cover the CDN hosts Crunchyroll has historically used. That was deliberately not done: speculative host permissions are both a security regression and a store-review liability, and adding them would have permanently widened the extension's reach to fix a problem it turned out not to have. One log line settled it — the real signed URL is `vod-fy-mod.crunchyrollcdn.com`, which already matched. The original text of this entry (below) is kept as written, since the suspicion was reasonable on the evidence available at the time.

**2026-07-26 (same day, superseded above) — The `*.crunchyrollcdn.com` host allowlist was never verified against a real signed subtitle URL, and is now the leading suspect for the English pipeline never working.** `isTrustedCrunchyrollCdnUrl` in `background.js` and the matching `host_permissions` entry in `manifest.json` both hardcode `*.crunchyrollcdn.com`. Nothing in the 2026-07-23 fact-finding record captures the hostname actually observed on the signed `.ass` URL in the `play` response — the host appears to have been assumed from the CDN's name rather than read off the URL. If Crunchyroll serves these from any other host (historically these have lived on `v.vrv.co`, `static.crunchyroll.com`, and various Akamai domains), the fetch is refused before it happens and the feature dies silently. Deliberately **not** fixed by broadening the allowlist to a guess: adding host permissions speculatively is both a security regression and a store-review liability, and the correct host is one console line away now that the sniffer logs it. Settled by the user reading the host off the new log line — see Open Questions.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — Diagnostic logging added at every stage of the English caption pipeline, rather than left silent.** Reversal of the implicit earlier choice to degrade quietly (the same "silently empty, like a missing frequency tag" pattern the audio and metadata fields use). That pattern is right for a field that is *optional*, but wrong for a whole feature whose only failure mode is "nothing appears": it let a completely non-functional pipeline pass a full live-testing round looking correct, because the English text on screen was coming from an unrelated source (the dual-language Jimaku file) and nothing distinguished that from our own pipeline working. The logging is unconditional rather than behind a debug flag — it is a handful of lines per episode load, and the cost of not having it has now been measured in a full testing cycle. Kept to the console rather than on-screen UI: an on-screen error would break the "keep them watching" positioning for something the user can't act on mid-episode.

**2026-07-26 (same day) — Dual-language Jimaku files confirmed as a real, already-flagged gap; reported rather than fixed.** While investigating the Translation-on-front report, confirmed empirically what `rankFiles`' own comment already flagged in the abstract: `parseAss` keeps every `Dialogue:` line and discards the Style field entirely, so a dual-language subtitle file feeds both languages into `cues`, and `updateJapaneseCue` joins everything matching the current timestamp into one line. Verified against the live Jimaku API: Frieren ep 1's KitaujiSub release carries `Text - CN(1160)` and `Text - JP(1151)` styles, and every Chinese line overlaps its Japanese counterpart, so the on-screen Japanese box and the Anki `Sentence` field (which *is* on the card front) both receive both languages. More directly relevant: **Witch Hat Atelier — this project's own main test show — auto-selects a dual-language file by default.** Its ep 1 has a `[Haruhana] … [CHS, JPN].ass` and a `[Haruhana] … [JPN].ass` from the same uploader; `rankFiles` ranks by uploader only, so both score equally and Jimaku's own ordering decides, putting the CHS+JPN file first. Deliberately **not fixed this session**: filtering by ASS style name, or by script detection per cue, or preferring a `[JPN]`-tagged filename over a `[CHS, JPN]` one, are three genuinely different designs with different failure modes, and picking one is a scope decision for the user rather than something to build proactively off a bug report about a different feature. Logged to Open Questions with those three options.

**2026-07-27 — Season resolution keyed on Crunchyroll's season name, not its number.** A live report on Re:Zero showed every season from the OVAs onward loading the next season's subtitles. The cause is that `partOfSeason.seasonNumber` is a positional index into Crunchyroll's own season list, which is ordered by release date and counts OVA/special collections as seasons — so the number is not the anime's season and never was. Three options were weighed. **Correcting the number arithmetically** (subtract however many non-season collections precede it) was rejected outright: the offset is per-show data this extension has no access to, so it would be a guess that happens to fix Re:Zero. **A manual per-show season override in the switcher panel** was rejected for now as a scope decision rather than a fix — it's a real escape hatch, but it makes the user do the work every time, and it should only be built if reading the page turns out to be impossible. **Matching on Crunchyroll's own season title** was chosen, because both sides already carry the same string: Crunchyroll names the season with its official English title and Jimaku indexes that same title as `english_name` (`"Re:ZERO -Starting Life in Another World- Season 2"` on both), so an exact match needs nothing inferred. Applied in two tiers — outright entry match, then the season number parsed out of the name — with the number kept as the last fallback so a show that publishes no season name behaves exactly as before. A season name identical to the series title is deliberately ignored rather than matched: that carries no season information, and matching on it would pin every season of every such show to the bare-franchise entry, i.e. season 1 — a regression for the multi-season shows the number path already handles. **Rests on one unverified assumption** (that the field exists at all), which is flagged in Open Questions and logged loudly at runtime rather than assumed silently.

**2026-07-27 — Sniffer replay on SPA navigation re-enabled.** That decision — don't replay a cached caption URL on episode change, because it may still be the previous episode's — was correct about the risk and turned out to be the cause of a bug: switching shows destroyed English cues that had *already* been correctly loaded for the new episode, because the pathname poll notices navigation up to a second after Crunchyroll has fetched and the sniffer has posted. The fix isn't to accept the risk but to remove it: the sniffer now stamps each URL with the pathname it was captured on, which is exactly the distinction that was missing, so a replay of the current episode's URL is accepted and a replay of the previous one is discarded. The alternative — polling the pathname faster to shrink the race — was rejected as making a real ordering problem statistically rarer rather than fixing it.

**2026-07-27 — Stale-state recovery moved to a periodic watchdog.** Both the `<video>` binding and the loaded episode identity were re-checked exactly once, at the instant a pathname change was noticed, on the assumption that everything else has settled by then. A show-to-show switch breaks that assumption — Crunchyroll rebuilds the player and its schema.org block on its own schedule — and the observed symptom was no subtitles at all until a hard reload. Considered extending the existing bounded retries (already 2 × 500ms) to cover a longer window; rejected as picking a number that is either too short for a slow switch or too long a delay for a fast one, and it would still be one bounded sequence rather than something that recovers whenever the page changes under us. A 2-second watchdog that re-checks both for the life of the page is what a full reload was doing by hand, minus the reload, and it self-heals cases nobody has reproduced yet. Guarded against firing on top of a load already in flight so it can't double-fetch.

**2026-07-27 — Emoji stripped by Unicode property, not by an enumerated list.** The 2026-07-05 markup rule listed three specific characters (`➡📺🎵`), which is why a loudspeaker emoji on a KonoSuba stage direction sailed through. Enumerating is the wrong shape for this: the set of pictographs a fansub might use is open-ended, and each addition costs a code change. Switched to `\p{Extended_Pictographic}`, which is exactly the "this is decoration, not language" class. Two carve-outs, both deliberate: `‼` and `⁉` are classified as pictographic by Unicode but are ordinary sentence-final punctuation in Japanese subtitles, so stripping them would delete real content; and `♪`/`〜` are not in the class at all, so the existing music-line and vowel-elongation handling is untouched. Same 2026-07-05 principle — meaningless symbols out, real punctuation left alone — with the boundary drawn by a Unicode property instead of by whichever characters had been noticed so far.

**2026-07-27 — English rendering slaved to the Japanese render's completion.** Live testing found the first subtitle after a page load or alt-tab appearing in English before Japanese, with everything after it simultaneous — the signature of a cold MV3 service worker, which the Japanese path needs (up to four `sendMessage` round-trips per line) and the English path doesn't. The obvious fix, keeping the worker warm with a periodic ping, was rejected: it fights the platform's own lifecycle for a symptom rather than removing the asymmetry, and it would still lose the race after a long enough idle. Instead the English box now waits for the Japanese line to actually be painted. This extends the 2026-07-26 pairing decision one step — that made a single decision drive *which* English text shows, this makes a single event drive *when* it shows — and it means any future slowness in the Japanese render can't reintroduce a stagger.

**2026-07-27 — Cue identity for audio widening switched from display text to cue timing.** The split-sentence audio widening located the end of a merged sentence by matching display text against the audio module's own cue timeline. Text is not a usable key: what gets recorded is the *join* of every cue visible at that instant, while the merge names *one* cue's text, so the two agree only when nothing else is on screen. Measured on Frieren ep 7, that's provider-dependent rather than rare — `[Moozzi2]` shows one cue at a time (0% of display windows have two or more), `[NanakoRaws]` shows two or more 80.5% of the time, and both are ordinary Japanese-only releases the switcher offers. Considered normalising the text on both sides so the keys match; rejected as making a fragile key slightly less fragile while leaving it dependent on filter behaviour staying identical across two modules. Timing is what the merge is actually computed from, is what the clip needs, and is unaffected by how a provider stacks its Dialogue events, so the timeline now records each entry's subtitle-file cue window and the widening matches on that.

**2026-07-27 — Duplicated subtitle display-filter chains collapsed into one function.** The filter chain (stage directions, speaker prefixes, inline furigana, fansub markup, half-width katakana) existed twice, verbatim, in `updateJapaneseCue` and `cueDisplayText` — the second carrying a comment promising it matched the first exactly, with nothing enforcing that. The same day's ordering fix (markup stripped before the stage-direction test) would have applied to one copy only and silently desynced what shows on screen from what a merged Anki sentence contains. Now one function called by both, which is what the comment was asking for.

**2026-07-29 — "Edit last card" hands off to Anki's own editor. (Superseded 2026-07-30.)** The build-order item said only "some way to jump into Anki's own card editor", so the design was open. Rejected outright: any in-page field editing, which would be a second and much worse Anki and is exactly what the scope guardrail against a competing review system exists to prevent. The real decision was WHERE the affordance lives. A button in the popup's success row alone was rejected as nearly useless in practice — the popup becomes a chip on the next subtitle line, i.e. within a second or two, and a typo on a card is noticed later than that. A persistent control alone was rejected too: the instant after capturing is when the user is most likely to want it. Both were built. **What this got wrong:** "an in-page editor would be a second, worse Anki" conflated two different things — building a rival *review system* (genuinely out of scope) with editing the fields of one card just created (not). Weighing only WHERE the button lives meant never asking what pressing it should *do*, and the answer it defaulted to — switch to the Anki desktop app — is itself an interruption of exactly the kind the litmus test exists to catch. The two trigger surfaces survive unchanged; only the action behind them was replaced.

**2026-07-29 — Filter and season rules defined by class, not by enumeration.** The user asked whether the 2026-07-27 fixes were general or fitted to the cases reported — a fair challenge, and auditing against real data found they were still partly fitted. The principle adopted: a rule should be stated as the class of thing it covers, and where that genuinely isn't possible, the enumeration should be labelled as deliberate rather than left looking like an oversight. Applied twice. **Markup:** the decorative-symbol rule is now `\p{Extended_Pictographic}` plus the contiguous U+2600–27BF span (Miscellaneous Symbols + Dingbats), which are whole Unicode blocks dedicated to decoration — chosen over adding the specific characters the corpus scan turned up (`♬➨⚟`), because that would have been the same enumeration one round later. The bracket pair `《》⸨⸩` stays an explicit list, and this is now documented as forced rather than lazy: Unicode gives `《》` the same categories as `「」『』（）`, so no property can separate a monologue marker from a real quote. **Seasons:** name matching tries three title forms rather than exact equality, because Crunchyroll and Jimaku don't agree on how much of the franchise title a season name repeats. Two exclusions were added from measurement, not intuition — geometric shapes (Japanese censors a character with `○`) and unit signs (`°℃№`, which Unicode also classes as symbols) — and `‼`/`⁉` stopped needing a special case at all once the rule keyed off symbol-vs-punctuation, which is what Unicode already encodes. Verified by measuring rather than reasoning: 514 changed lines out of 49,592 real corpus cues with nothing correct broken, and five previously-unresolvable seasons across three shows now resolving.

**2026-07-29 — Cour-split entries retried on empty, not merged into season selection.** Jimaku splits a two-cour season into separate `Part N` entries while Crunchyroll presents it as one continuous season, so episodes past the cour boundary had no files in the entry chosen for the season and failed outright (confirmed live: Re:Zero's "2nd Season" entry has zero files for episodes 14–25). Considered merging every same-season entry's file list up front; rejected because it doubles the request count on every single load to serve the minority of episodes past a boundary, and it would blur which entry a file actually came from in the switcher panel — which exists precisely so the resolved entry is visible. Retrying siblings only when the chosen entry returns nothing costs an extra request on exactly the episodes that would otherwise have errored, and leaves the normal path untouched.

**2026-07-29 — English gap-bridging gated on English-text identity, not on the timer.** Built to the user's own written spec (three zones by gap length). The one thing the spec left open was the Zone 2/3 cutoff, explicitly flagged as needing tuning rather than a guessed constant, with a suggestion that it might need to be relative to cue duration. Measured 14,742 gaps across 24 real files before choosing: the distribution has no trough (78% of gaps under 100ms, then smooth decay), and it varies much more by PROVIDER than by show — 43% vs 78% sub-100ms on two releases of the *same episode*. So neither a fixed nor a duration-scaled threshold can separate "same sentence" from "next sentence". The resolution is that it doesn't have to: bridging already requires both flanking cues to pair with the same English cue, which is what establishes they're one sentence, so the threshold's only job is to stop the English line floating alone under an empty Japanese box through a long pause. Set to 1.0s as a working default and logged as tunable-by-eye rather than derivable. Also worth recording because the spec asked: making English visibility depend on Japanese timing was **not** a new dependency — the 2026-07-26 pairing fix had already made English entirely Japanese-window-driven, so this hooked into existing shared state, and the spec's Zone 1 was already implemented.

**2026-07-30 — "Edit last card" rebuilt as an in-page panel.** The first implementation used `guiEditNote`/`guiBrowse` as the primary action, which requires leaving the browser and fails the "capture and keep watching" litmus test. AnkiConnect's `notesInfo`/`updateNoteFields` don't require Anki's GUI to be focused or even visible, so an in-page panel achieves the same result without the interruption. Anki's own editor is kept as a secondary "Open in Anki" escape hatch, scoped to edits outside this panel's coverage (tags, note type, deletion) — not removed, just demoted.

**2026-07-30 — Word/Reading/Gloss are edited via dictionary re-lookup, not free-text boxes.** These three fields are cascade-derived from a single dictionary-entry selection at capture time, so editing them as independent text fields would let them drift out of sync with each other and with POS/Frequency/JLPT. Rejected free-text editing in favour of reopening the same tokenize→lookup UI used at capture: the stored JP sentence is shown with the captured word's span highlighted, the user can pick a different sense for that span or click a different token entirely, and the selection cascades all five derived fields together through the existing pipeline rather than a second one.

**2026-07-30 — POS/Frequency/JLPT/Source are read-only in the edit panel.** These are deterministic outputs of the dictionary entry and episode metadata, not independently authored at capture time. Allowing direct override would create a state where these fields could contradict the Word/Gloss selection they're supposed to describe. They display live and update automatically when a new dictionary entry is picked via the Word/Reading/Gloss re-lookup, but are never directly typed. One implementation consequence worth recording: POS/Frequency/JLPT are opt-in toggles at capture time, so a card captured with a toggle off carries them empty deliberately — re-picking a word therefore only rewrites each of them if it was already populated, rather than smuggling metadata onto a card that was deliberately created without it.

**2026-07-30 — Audio trim editor works from a retained in-memory PCM buffer.** Re-decoding the already-exported clip (`retrieveMediaFile`) would only support tightening the clip, never recovering audio outside the originally captured bounds. Real cases exist for both directions — tightening (cutting bleed from an adjacent line, trimming dead air) and extending (audio started late and needs more lead-in, or the user wants to include the next line for context). The buffer's limited lifetime (dies on full page reload) was evaluated and found to be a non-issue: both entry points to the editor are already bounded to lifetimes shorter than or equal to the buffer's — the persistent control's `nid` itself already clears on reload, so there was never a scenario where the buffer needed to outlive it. Stored un-normalized, so re-trimming the same clip repeatedly can't compound the loudness gain.

**2026-07-30 — PCM buffer padded beyond the captured clip, to allow extending.** A buffer trimmed to the exact original capture bounds would only support tightening, defeating the purpose of the previous decision. A few seconds of padding on each side is captured alongside the clip specifically to make extension possible. Exact padding duration is a tuning parameter, not fixed here — see Open Questions. **Found while building:** post-roll cannot exist at the moment a clip is finalized, because the cue has only just ended and nothing after it has been recorded yet. Retention therefore happens twice — immediately, so the editor works with pre-roll only if opened at once, and again once enough time has passed for the post-roll to exist — rather than delaying the clip's own export to wait for it.

**2026-07-30 — Card-content discovery moved from the popup to a two-state chip.** The popup was evaluated as a display surface for verifying captured content and rejected for three independent reasons: its layout is sized for dictionary lookup, not a sentence-level summary; its "Adding…" → "Added to Anki" transition is gated on audio-capture completion, not subtitle timing, so it's frequently still in a pending state when the subtitle line itself changes; and some subtitle lines (single-word lines especially) don't stay on screen long enough for any popup-based verification window to be reliable. The chip's lifetime is already decoupled from the subtitle line's lifetime, which resolves the third problem for free, and it only appears once export is actually complete, which resolves the second. *(The second reason was removed at the source on 2026-07-31 — the Anki send is now two-phase, so nothing waits on audio capture. The decision stands on its other two grounds, and the chip now appears when the card is written rather than when its audio lands.)*

**2026-07-30 — Chip verification view excludes POS/Frequency/JLPT/Source.** These four fields are exactly the ones covered by the project's known bug categories (segmentation, sense selection, desync, merge glitches) and are what's actually worth spending a short glance-check window on. POS/Frequency/JLPT/Source are deterministic outputs of a correct Word/Gloss selection and are rarely independently wrong, so including them would spend limited attention and limited panel space on low-value verification. This also decouples the verification view from the real Anki card-face layout, which is expected to change in Phase 6 — the verification view doesn't need to track that redesign.

**2026-07-30 — Chip auto-expand duration scales with content length.** A single fixed duration would be too short for longer captured sentences and unnecessarily long for single-word captures. Exact scaling curve deferred to tuning during live-testing — see Open Questions.

**2026-07-30 — Chip hover-expand smoothing/morph animation deferred to Phase 6.** The chip system currently shows/hides instantly regardless of mouse position, consistent with the rest of the current UI's visual polish level. A smoother morph between collapsed and expanded states was raised but explicitly deferred to Phase 6, when the broader visual/UI redesign happens, rather than built as a one-off ahead of that pass.

**2026-07-30 — Render pipeline extracted into `buildGroupsForText` for reuse.** The edit panel's "Change word" flow needs the stored sentence tokenized exactly as the subtitle line was at capture time, or a word could segment differently in the panel than it did on screen. Rebuilding that pipeline for the panel would have been a second copy of a four-stage async chain — the same duplication that had to be undone for the display filters on 2026-07-27, and with a worse failure mode, since a drift here would silently produce a different word. Extracted to one awaited function used by both. Behaviour change is confined to superseded lines now finishing their group-building before being discarded rather than bailing mid-chain: a few wasted dictionary lookups on rapid subtitle changes, and the generation check that actually matters still guards the DOM write. Verified equivalent rather than assumed — `scripts/test-render-pipeline.js` runs the new function and a frozen copy of the old chain over 794 real subtitle lines from two providers and compares the groups field by field.

---

**2026-07-31 — Bolding the target word is markup, never a text edit.** The edit panel's sentence rebuild took an offset on trust and spliced the new word in at it, which is only ever correct while the offset really does spell that word. Changing the target word broke that assumption directly (the old word's position, the new word's length) and overwrote part of the sentence. The alternative considered was passing the correct offset and leaving the splice — rejected, because the same class of bug returns the moment any caller's offset goes stale for any other reason. The offset is now a hint that is verified against the text before use, with a search and then no-bolding as fallbacks, so the function cannot alter the sentence's own characters under any input. The user's exact reported string is a regression test.

**2026-07-31 — The Anki card is sent before its audio, not with it.** A capture used to wait for the subtitle line to finish before writing anything, because the clip is sliced from the completed cue — and for a sentence split across two cues that means waiting out the second half, which the live pass reported as a multi-second delay before the capture chip appeared. Shortening the wait was the obvious alternative and is the wrong direction: a wait that expires produces first-half-only audio on the card, which is the same report's other half. The note is now written immediately with every text field, and a second `updateNoteFields` call attaches the audio when it's ready. That decouples the two, so the wait can be *longer* (20s) rather than shorter, and the verification chip appears at once. Cost: a card exists for a moment without its audio, and a capture undone in that window must not be written to afterwards — tracked by note id rather than by "is this still the last card", since a second capture also moves that pointer.

**2026-07-31 — A superseded audio graph is silenced, not disconnected.** Crunchyroll swapping in a new `<video>` built a second capture graph while the first kept running, and both wrote into the same ring buffer — two interleaved streams, which is what the reported waveform corruption was. Disconnecting the old graph is the obvious fix and is specifically forbidden here: dropping a `createMediaElementSource` connection silences the page's audio until a full reload (confirmed 2026-07-17, by accident). Each graph now carries a generation number and writes only while it is the current one. Nothing is disconnected, no audio routing changes, and the stale graph simply stops contributing.

**2026-07-31 — Merged clip edges come from subtitle timings, not display windows.** A displayed window spans every cue visible at that instant, so on a provider that stacks cues (measured: [NanakoRaws] shows two or more 80.5% of the time) it routinely begins before the merged sentence and ends after it. Bounding the clip by those edges therefore over-captures by construction. The window is still what locates the sentence in the ring buffer — that mapping exists nowhere else — but the edges are now interpolated back to the sentence's own timings, clamped to the window they were measured from. Rejected alternative: matching cues by text, which is what this replaced on 2026-07-27 and cannot work when the displayed text is a join of several cues.

**2026-07-31 — An unidentified Jimaku entry asks rather than guesses.** Films, OVAs, specials and compilations have no `partOfSeason` block on Crunchyroll's page, so none of the season-matching tiers can fire and resolution fell through to the plain title match — which for a film listed under a franchise page is the franchise's own entry, i.e. season 1's subtitles, chosen silently. Every diagnostic in the path happened to be conditional on a mismatch being *detected*, so the case with no season data to disagree about produced an empty console. Two changes: entry resolution is logged on every load, and a title that can't be positively identified is marked unconfident, which surfaces an entry picker in the switcher panel listing every candidate the search returned. The alternative — keep guessing but log it — was rejected because the guess is wrong often enough to need a fix, not just a note, and the panel already existed as the place to fix it. Deliberately strict about what counts as identification: an entry name *contained in* Crunchyroll's title is the franchise, not the film, and accepting that direction of match would reintroduce the exact bug.

**2026-07-31 — Jimaku searches widen instead of failing.** Jimaku's search is a substring match against its own titles, so a spelling it doesn't hold returns nothing at all — which is how four titles that demonstrably exist in the catalogue came back as "No Jimaku entry found" in the live pass. A zero-result search now re-tries on progressively broader queries (the segment after the last colon, then trailing words dropped one at a time), capped at six rungs and only ever reached when the previous rung found nothing. Rejected alternative: treating a zero result as genuinely absent and leaning on the manual-upload fallback, which is a real dead end for content Jimaku actually covers. Every rung and every match rule is measured against live API responses rather than reasoned about.

**2026-08-01 — "Is this episodic TV?" is asked of the season NAME, not of whether Crunchyroll published a season block.** The 2026-07-31 work treated a missing `partOfSeason` block as the definition of non-episodic content. That proxy is wrong in both directions, and a fixture-based suite found three separate live failures resting on it. Crunchyroll lists OVA/OAD collections *as* seasons, block and all, so they went down the season-number path: Attack on Titan's OADs took **season 2's entry silently**, from a populated entry, so the subtitles looked entirely plausible. The same misreading suppressed the unfiltered-file fallback, so Re:Zero's OVAs resolved to exactly the right entry and then hard-errored with "No subtitle file found for episode 1" — an entry holding four files, none of them numbered 1. And in the other direction, a film has no block at all, so `wantedSeason` defaulted to 1 and the season match then claimed credit for it: `matched by season 1` logged on a movie, which is the one thing that log line exists to make impossible. All three now branch on a format classifier read from the season name (`nonEpisodicClass`), plus a tier that matches Crunchyroll's format against a Jimaku entry's own — which is what connects Crunchyroll's "OAD" to Jimaku's "…OVA". **OVA/OAD/ONA are deliberately one class**: they are the same format under different names, and Jimaku indexes Attack on Titan's OADs under "OVA". The rejected alternative was fixing the three symptoms where they surfaced — a special case for OVA-named seasons, a widened file-lookup fallback, a corrected log string — which would have left the same wrong question being asked in four places. Ambiguity degrades rather than guesses: a franchise with several side formats of the same class matches none of them and falls through to the entry picker.

**2026-08-01 — The no-valid-entry warning state loads nothing, not a nearest guess.** When the entry picker has no correct answer — a recap film like AoT Chronicle, which has no Jimaku entry of its own at all — the extension no longer auto-loads a nearest plausible entry behind the warning banner. Rejected alternative: keep loading the nearest plausible entry (in practice the franchise's season 1) while the warning is up. Rejected because a missed banner risks capturing wrong-show sentences into **permanent Anki cards**, which is a worse failure than a temporarily blank subtitle track — the card outlives the mistake, the blank track doesn't. Playback itself is never blocked; only subtitle loading stops. The line is drawn at the `entries[0]` fallback specifically: every other tier is an actual match against the title, whereas `entries[0]` is "whatever the search returned first". So a title that matches an entry still loads even if the requested season doesn't exist on Jimaku (guarded by its own fixture) — this is not "blank the screen on any warning". **Consequence:** every tier that can select an entry also identifies it, so `confident` is true for anything the resolver returns. That let the computed `confident` and the "using X as a guess" warning it gated be deleted outright — both were only reachable through the `entries[0]` fallback. The suite asserts the resulting invariant (an entry is returned iff it was identified), because the whole change rests on it.

The switcher's *other* warning variant — "check these are the right subtitles", shown over loaded files — was briefly assessed as unreachable too and is **not**: `renderSwitcherOptions` is also called from the entry-picker's own change handler, where the user has just picked an entry and something IS loaded. That is precisely the state the wording is for, and removing it would have made the panel claim "no subtitles were loaded" over subtitles the user had just chosen. Reachability here has to be judged across both call sites, not from the resolver's return values alone.

**2026-08-01 — Non-episodic content is identified by its OWN title, and a film that can't be named isn't guessed at.** The first live pass to read the *service worker* console (resolver logging lives there, not in the page console — earlier rounds saw none of it) found three films failing three different ways, all from one gap: nothing in resolution ever read the episode's own title, so there was no signal saying WHICH film a page was. Mugen Train loaded **Infinity Castle's** subtitles, confidently and silently — the worst shape yet, since the previous session's work made it *look* resolved. Infinity Castle's own page refused correctly but by accident (a trailing "I" in Crunchyroll's title made the correct single search result read as a near-miss). The Last Attack fell through to the flagship TV series entry. Measured against the live API, the root cause is that **the search never looked for the film**: "Demon Slayer: Kimetsu no Yaiba" returns six entries and Mugen Train's is not among them, so no matching rule over those results could have found it, while "Mugen Train" finds it first. Three coordinated changes: `content.js` now reads `name` (the episode title) off the same JSON-LD block; for non-episodic content the resolver runs an **additional** search on that title and merges the results (the existing ladder can't help — it only widens when a rung returns *nothing*, and a franchise query returns plenty); and a new top-priority `matchEntryByContentTitle` tier identifies the entry by it, breaking ties by format class ("Mugen Train" matches both the film and the TV retelling, and the class says which). **Films must be identified by title, collections need not be:** a franchise routinely has several films, so "the only film-class entry in these results" is whichever film the search happened to return — exactly the Mugen Train failure — whereas a franchise has one OVA bucket and one specials bucket, where the format word IS the identity and is what makes Crunchyroll's "OADs" resolve to Jimaku's "…OVA". So a movie with no title evidence now resolves to nothing and asks, rather than falling through to a sibling film or to the franchise's TV entry. Rejected alternative: patching each of the three symptoms where it surfaced, which would have left the missing signal missing.

**Refined once the live JSON-LD arrived (same day).** Two things it settled. First, the original failure was **purely** a search-coverage failure: `partOfSeason.name` on that page is already the specific film title ("Demon Slayer -Kimetsu no Yaiba- The Movie: Mugen Train"), which `matchEntryBySeasonName` matches exactly — so the season-name tier had the right data all along and failed only because the film's entry was never among the candidates. The log line that read `for "Demon Slayer: Kimetsu no Yaiba"` is `partOfSeries.name` (the resolver's `query`), not the season name, which is what made it look like the season signal was missing. With the extra search in place, Mugen Train now resolves via the season name, and the content-title tier is the backstop for pages like The Last Attack where no season name exists. Second, JSON-LD's `name` is **not** a title but a compound — `"X | E1 - X"`, pipe included — and passing it to Jimaku verbatim returns **zero** results (measured). It is now split back into the titles it is built from (the part before the pipe, and the part after the `E<n> -` marker) and joined by `partOfSeason.name` as a third candidate, most-specific-first. The two halves differ for OVA collections, where the post-marker half is the one naming the individual OVA — which is what the file narrowing needs. Every fixture now carries the verbatim compound rather than a cleaned title, since a clean one would pass without testing the parsing at all.

**Corrected again once Memory Snow's page arrived (same day), and the lesson is about the fixtures, not the parser.** Its real `name` is `"OVAs | EEX - Memory Snow (Director's Cut)"` — a different shape from the film page the parser was built against, and it broke on both halves. The episode code is **`EEX`, not numeric**, so an `E\d+` marker extracted nothing at all and left only the pre-pipe `"OVAs"` — which names a format, not a work, and narrows nothing; measured, the feature silently did not fire. And the title carries a `(Director's Cut)` qualifier Jimaku's filenames don't have, so even a parsed code would not have matched. Three changes: the episode code is now matched as an **opaque token** (`E` plus any short alphanumeric run — Crunchyroll uses several beyond `E1`/`EEX`), anything after a pipe is taken even when no code matches at all, so the next format degrades to a usable title rather than to nothing; a trailing-qualifier-stripped variant is added as an **extra** candidate so the fuller title still gets first go; and a candidate that is nothing but a format word is dropped, for the same reason one equal to the series title is. **This is the second time a fixture built on an inferred shape passed while the real page failed** — the standing rule is now that any Crunchyroll-derived string in a fixture is either captured verbatim from a live page or explicitly marked as a guess.

**And a third time, which is what finally settled the mechanism (same day).** The captured pages for Infinity Castle and The Last Attack show a shape neither marker-based case predicted: `name` is the title **duplicated across the pipe with no episode code at all**. Both films' season names also carry no format word ("Infinity Castle I", "THE LAST ATTACK"), so `nonEpisodicClass` saw nothing and both were classed as ordinary TV — which skipped the entire content-title path and reproduced both original bugs exactly, on fixtures that had been passing on invented metadata. Two additions. The duplication is now itself the signal (`looksLikeStandaloneWork`): an episodic page names the series on one side of the pipe and the episode on the other, so a page naming the same work twice is a standalone work. And a single-result search is trusted as an identification (`soloHit`) — the principle `matchEntryByFullTitle` already applied to a full-query hit, extended to the searches made here, because Jimaku's entry name puts "the Movie:" in the *middle* of "Attack on Titan the Movie: The Last Attack" and no containment test can reach it. It is ordered below the format match (an episode-title search can return one unrelated show) and above the exact-title match (whose match on a bare franchise name is the flagship-series fallback), and is guarded by a franchise check that runs in **both** directions — the entry name extends the series for a film folded into its parent show, and is *shorter* than it for a film with its own Crunchyroll series entity.

**The two arrangements are a real distinction, not a quirk:** Infinity Castle has its own Crunchyroll series entity, so `partOfSeries.name` is already the film title; The Last Attack is folded into the parent show, so it is the bare franchise. That is precisely why the two originally failed *differently* — a rejected near-miss versus an exact-match fall-through to the flagship series — and both arrangements now have their own verbatim fixture. Also confirmed while checking this: `seasonNumber: 0` is a real value (Infinity Castle) and resolution handles it correctly — every test is `!== null` or `??`, never falsy. The one `&&` on it is a display string that omits "Season 0" from the Anki show/episode label, which is right for a film.

**2026-08-01 — The non-episodic file fallback narrows by episode title before dumping everything.** An OVA collection's files are one per OVA, so listing all of them showed Memory Snow and The Frozen Bond the identical four-file list — the switcher offering no disambiguating information at all. The unfiltered listing is now filtered by the episode's own title first, on the same punctuation-insensitive key used for entry titles so release-name separators don't defeat it. **Only a match narrows:** Crunchyroll's "The Frozen Bond" and the uploader's "Hyouketsu no Kizuna" share nothing, so that case matches none and correctly falls through to the full list rather than to an empty one — asserted by its own fixture, since narrowing to zero would be worse than not narrowing.

**2026-08-01 — The CMS's missing episode numbers are a CMS artifact, not a runtime one.** The second sweep flagged 13 entries with no `episode_number`, the Gundam films among them, and the question was whether that made a whole class undetectable. It doesn't: JSON-LD reports `episodeNumber: 0` for Cucuruz Doan's Island where the CMS reports `null`, and JSON-LD is the field the extension actually reads. The two surfaces disagree here in the *opposite* direction to the One Piece case — which is the point, and why this was measured rather than inferred from the CMS a second time. Verified by running the page's verbatim metadata end to end: it resolves through the existing film path (duplicated-title-no-marker → non-episodic → lone-result match), entry 2034, three files, and the episode filter is never asked. A full audit of the episode path for falsy-zero handling found none — every site is `??` or `Number.isInteger`, never `||`, so a 0 is carried where a `null` would be rejected. Pinned by a fixture that also asserts **no `?episode=` request is made**, since asking it on a film is precisely what the non-episodic gate exists to prevent.

**2026-08-01 — Second catalogue sweep (45 series, 75 seasons, 2,385 episodes), weighted to franchises with many sub-series.** Gundam, Fate, Dragon Ball, Detective Conan and Code Geass — archived at `fixtures/crunchyroll-catalogue-2026-08-01b.json`. **Zero classification misses and zero review candidates**, which is the main result: the season-title classifier held across a catalogue slice deliberately chosen to break it. Three structural findings, none of them a silent-wrong-content bug:

**Crunchyroll publishes real, watchable entries with no episode number at all** — 13 in this sample: the Gundam films (Char's Counterattack, Awakening of the Trailblazer, SEED Freedom, Narrative, Cucuruz Doan's Island), prologues carrying the code `"0"` with `episode_number: null`, and PVs. Detection previously rejected a page on `episodeNumber` *before* reading the title code, so a prologue whose code says `0` was discarded along with the number. The guard now runs after the code is read. **The films remain unresolvable**: they have neither a number nor a code, and detection still bails with "Couldn't detect the show/episode". Deliberately not fixed by defaulting the number, because whether their JSON-LD matches the CMS on this field is exactly what today's One Piece finding showed cannot be assumed — see Open Questions.

**The CMS's own code-vs-number split matches the JSON-LD one**, checked across all 2,385 episodes: 2,356 agree, 4 disagree (all the `"0"`/null prologues), 7 carry non-numeric codes (`SP1`, `P1`, `SP`, and Heaven's Feel's `I`/`II`/`III`). That the two fields diverge in the same shape on both surfaces is corroboration for preferring the code, not just a One Piece quirk. Now a standing check in the analyzer.
